ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U78	Thank you. This is a reduced fine, from EUR 320 to EUR 200. The previous penalty rate was disproportionately high for smaller companies and non-profit organisations. The determination of the fine shall be, in particular, the discretion of the registrar. In the past, registrars decided not to impose a large fine, as it was often unlikely to be reached. As a result of the amendment, revenue from fines in the state budget is not expected to decrease; rather, smaller fines can be more easily recovered. That is also the reason for this amendment.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U90	Mr. President! Ladies and gentlemen, We have another draft law to amend the law. We keep changing and changing. It's like we're making more work for ourselves. I would say that for some reason society expects us to have every move regulated by law, as if there were no customs in Estonia, as if there were no traditions in Estonia. We want to regulate everything by law. Recently, we discussed a draft law amending the Law on Animal Protection. According to this, pigs must be asked how they can be slaughtered and whether it is humane or animated or whatever. Now we're gonna go through the business code again and we're gonna work it out. The Commercial Code, of course, is a very important and big law. Most of us who work in the Riigikogu have been exposed to the Commercial Code. Personally, I've been in contact with the business register. You're gonna make some sort of entry there (in other words, it's normal), you're gonna do a regular act, for example, you're gonna have to change the membership of a non-profit organization, but it always ends up being the case because it wasn't the right thing, some digital signature was in the wrong place. It's all been made so complicated! At all, life's laws have been complicated. In some places, it could be a lot easier. But now we are going to change the law, which, frankly, is extremely specific. Most of us who sit here have read it, but still don't understand everything. Lawyers have to deal with these things and lawyers have to trust more. There are errors in many of the draft laws that we have seen here in the Riigikogu precisely during the night sittings: errors of writing, language errors, legal errors. I don't understand whether the bill has been a problem with lawyers or whether the legal correction has been done by vets or anyone. If we have a draft law amending the law, there will never be any assurance that it will not be followed in about six months' time by a draft law amending the law and studying the law. That's how we keep changing these laws here. But in fact, there are very important things in this law. It's either that department is allowed to make entries from other departments. But still, we're changing the law, but we're not gonna finish this thing. It will be extended, yes, the responsibility of the departments, but there is still no clarity as to who will be responsible if any mistake has been made and who will correct it. Or is it still the unfortunate soul who wanted to make some sort of entry, gets procedural information and has to surf again in a business register environment that is quite difficult and complicated, especially for someone who doesn't do computer hack every day? Of course, it is to be welcomed that in this draft law amending the other laws there is at last also talk about internal auditors. This is, I would say, an event with a signicant meaning right now and here, because as far as I have seen the laws here in my short parliamentary career, there has still been no mention of internal auditors or internal control, which is a very interesting subject. I'll have three more minutes, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U91	Are you sure about this? Three minutes extra time.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U92	This time, of course, is very short. In three minutes, you won't be able to unravel such a wide-ranging issue as internal control. Internal control is, in fact, a very important management mechanism. The internal audit is an activity that passes on internal control systems to an institution or an undertaking. Today, the internal auditor is the first assistant to the system's top manager; he is an extension of the system's top manager's arm. Without a proper internal audit, neither a large business corporation, state institution nor a local government institution can operate. If we look at this draft law amending other laws, we will see that the time limits for the examinations required when applying for the professional level of the internal auditor have been extended. It's a good thing they're being extended. But are there any incentives for auditors to acquire knowledge? Is there something being done to improve and improve the internal control system in public authorities and local authorities? No, I don't see it. All I see is that the deadlines are being extended. I mean, the signal is that internal audit can continue to linger, nobody cares if the internal control system is effective, it gives the chief executive full assurance that no errors, no irregularities or fraud will occur. After all, this is one of the most important tasks of the internal control system to prevent irregularities and fraud. One of the most important activities of the internal auditor is, for example, the audit of irregularities and fraud, which is extremely interesting. I know this because I myself have run an internal audit unit in Tallinn. By the way, it was the first local authority to introduce an internal control and internal audit system. After the internal audit unit had been implemented in Tallinn, it was taken as an example elsewhere. I was honoured to advise on the establishment of such units in Pärnu, Tartu and Võru. This is a very important matter. But here's just an extension of the deadlines and that's it. Is this bill the way we want it? I don't know. But that's a good thing. We look forward to the time when we will begin to amend, correct and improve the law on the amendment of other laws. Well, there's probably gonna be a chance, because there might be more night sessions. It's actually a really great undertaking, a night hearing. You can read the bill properly, come up with questions, and if there is an opportunity and there are attentive listeners, you can also give a small speech and say all your thoughts that have crept into your heart. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U106	The second reading of the draft law amending the matrimonial property register law, the notary fee law and the Civil Status Acts Act initiated by the Estonian Reform Party Group and the Group of the Isamaa and Res Publica Union begins. I would like to ask Siim Kabritsa, Member of the Committee on Legal Affairs, to serve as a waitress.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U107	Dear Chairman of the Riigikogu! Ladies and gentlemen, At its meeting on 15 November, the Riigikogu Legal Affairs Committee discussed the draft law amending the matrimonial property register law, the notary's fee law and the Civil Status Act 308. Among the members of the Centre Party, Marika Tuus-Laul and Peeter Võsa, among others, took part in the meeting of the Commission. Mr Kalle Laane was absent from the members of the Commission. Berit Loog, Adviser to the Ministry of Justice's Office for Legislative Affairs and Development, was invited to the debate. The purpose of the draft is to create the prerequisites for the transition to an electronic matrimonial property register, the introduction of electronic registers and files and the facilitation of access to data entered in the matrimonial property register. The paper register will no longer be completed, the processing of submitted applications will become faster and the processing software will exchange data with other information systems. The basic data included in the register can be consulted quickly and easily. The main changes to the draft are as follows: Prerequisites for the transition to an electronic matrimonial property register will be created, better access to the data of the electronic matrimonial property register will be created, the regulation of the electronic matrimonial property register will be clarified, and the procedure for submitting data on selected property relationships to the matrimonial property register will be simplified. The Commission decided to put the draft on the agenda of the plenary session on 20 November 2012, to propose the termination of the second reading and, if the second reading is closed, to send the draft to the third reading. All decisions were taken by consensus. Thank you.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U108	Thank you very much, Mr Kabrits. Let's start asking questions. Mr Eldar Efendiev, please!

I also want to defend the idea of Siim Kabritsa that it is probably a very good law. At least for history. A good colleague, Peeter Võsa, said last night out of this chair a fruitful idea that Parliament's task is not strictly to write or adopt one law or another, but rather to carry out much wider activities of lawmaking. This means building up a system of laws that are harmoniously compatible with each other. Our good colleague, Professor Igor Gräzin, has described this matter even more philosophically and has mentioned that legislation is, in addition, part of national culture, especially legal culture. It is lawmaking that is characterised by the level of development, legal thinking or thinking ability of one country or another. I hope that I remember this point made by Mr Gräzin precisely, but if I was wrong about something, nothing bad has happened, because the name of a good colleague has been mentioned and he will certainly be able to clarify his legal philosophy. In any case, this episode of legislation remains in history. However, let us come back to the concrete draft that we are dealing with. This is a bill that I, as a historian, consider to be very important. The point is that, in addition to a legal philosophy, a pending bill will help to leave another strong mark in the history of human and mentality. It is the various registers of property that are now one of the most important sources of history, and they have been there for centuries. They allow historians to explore the history of the lost generation of economics, domesticity, mentality, and also thinking. Although we may not like it, sooner or later we too are among those generations who have gone down in history. The registers, which allow us to address the history of our peasantry in a colorful way, are, for example, registers of the property of dead hosts. They're still in the archives. These registers have vigorously changed our perception of the everyday life of our ancestors, perhaps adding many colors to the grey picture so far. It says, for example, all the harnessed animals. You read and enjoy the thought of this peasant nation. There you will find the oral portraits of the pussies and the pussies, what they looked like and what the performance of one or the other was. Tools have been described, such as the fact that while fishing with the sting or with the shunt was strictly forbidden, the son was left with such important tools as his inheritance. We also know that a serf peasant should not have a weapon, because possession of a weapon is only the hallmark of a free man. At the same time, we find in the 1703 property registers that one in ten farmers had a gun on the wall. Maybe there wasn't any servitude. At least that is what Alexander Loit, an Estonian historian in Sweden, says that at the end of the Swedish era we actually lost our serfdom. These examples, of course, could be infinite. But I'm asking for three more minutes!
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U186	Three minutes.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U187	I'll give you a few more examples. We all think that in the mid-19th century, the schoolmaster was one of the noble lords of the village society. We'll read his property records and find out he had 20 pieces of furniture. This included a bed, a chair, a table, a pang, and a portrait of the 16 cheap emperors printed. Turns out he wasn't a very wealthy man after all. The property registers tell of contacts with distant lands. We can see what inventory the craftsmen had. These records also speak of spiritual life. Because in the property registers, you know, things like books are interesting. If people had these books, we might think they probably read them. In turn, we can deduce what ideas spread in one or other part of the population or in one or other city among different professions and positions. You know, this stuff is already exciting. At times, it gives us a vastly educated, richer, and brighter picture of our ancestors than we are accustomed to reading from a book. Another example. Professor Ivanovsky's estate records read that he too had a huge fortune. The main part of it was 10,000 items, 10,000 empty bottles of beer. And so on. That's all I said about records from old times. But to you, ladies and gentlemen, I would like to express my pleasure that there will be such an exciting source of history as the matrimonial property register. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U266	We will go to the next item on the agenda, which is the second reading of the draft law amending the Public Information Act, initiated by the Government of the Republic. I would like a presentation by Andrei Korobeinik, member of the Constitutional Committee of the Chair.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U267	President-in-Office of the Council. Ladies and gentlemen, The Committee on Constitutional Affairs discussed the draft at 263 two sittings: s. October 22nd and November 5th. During the first reading, it became clear that the various parties, namely the Ministry of Justice, MKM, ITL and the Internet community, have quite different views on the draft. That is what we discussed at the working group meeting, which took place outside the work schedule of the Committee on Constitutional Affairs. No amendments to the draft were received within the deadline, but the Committee on Constitutional Affairs decided to initiate the four amendments itself as a result of the discussions. All parties concerned were satisfied with the final text of the draft. Let me remind you that the purpose of the draft is to make public information re-usable, and I will give you more details on the proposals. Firstly, the information disclosed by the State must be up-to-date, i.e. it is not enough to publish the data; it must also be updated. Secondly, the Government of the Republic will have the opportunity to establish rules for making information available from where and how the data can be retrieved, for example from the information gate of eesti.ee. Thirdly, unlike the previous version, the state must publish the data free of charge according to the text of the new draft. New databases will have to be free of charge from the moment they are created, while the old will be allowed a three-year transitional period. The deadline is 1 January 2015. This is the most important change. It is important to mention that although the state can no longer take money for using the data, it can still take money for services. For example, companies' data is basic data, while the business register is a service. However, the remuneration for services should be subject to regulations, similar to state fees. At present, this is not a common practice. Since basic data is free of charge, some private companies can create a more convenient or more favourable service and compete with it. The consensual proposal of the Committee on Constitutional Affairs was to put the draft on the plenary agenda on 21 November today it will be in this Chamber at second reading and the second reading will be closed. If the second reading is completed, send the draft to the third reading by 5 December. Thank you. I look forward to hearing your questions.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U303	No further questions for the rapporteur. Thank you very much, Andrei Korobeinik. Do you want to open negotiations? Please, Priit Tobal!
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U304	Good chairman of the sitting! Ladies and gentlemen, The draft law amending the Public Information Act aims to stimulate the creation of new products and services in the private sector by making public information re-usable. To this end, the Estonian legal order is specified by the provisions on the re-use of public information. I will stop briefly on how this bill reached our tables. Its initiation was due to the need to improve the infringements referred to by the European Commission in the Estonian legal order. The Commission has indicated that Estonia does not guarantee the right to re-use public information or that solutions in current law are not sufficiently legal. It can therefore be reiterated that the wider purpose of the draft is to resolve the problems referred to by the European Commission and to lay down more clearly the rules for the re-use of public information in the Estonian legal order. As the Minister for Economic Affairs and Communications told us here at first reading of this draft, the law complements the concept of re-use of public information. Some of the public information shall be made available in a machine-readable form for reuse and availability. The draft also complements the principle of costorientation, which requires the holder of information to provide explanations on the development of charges for access to and re-use of public information. I would like to mention here that the charges to be charged must not be too restrictive and unduly anti-competitive. In order to calculate the costs, a clearer principle has been given whereby revenue from recovery must not exceed the costs of re-use plus a reasonable depreciation cost to ensure the sustainability of the service. In this context, it is important to pay attention to paragraph 2 of the draft. It complements the Public Information Act with § 31, in which the term "re-use of public information" is broadly delineated: "Re-use of public information by a natural or legal person for commercial or non-commercial purposes is the use of public information which does not coincide with the original purpose for which this information was received or created in the performance of public tasks. 'For the purpose of re-use of information, the holder of information may not enter into exclusive agreements unless necessary and justified by the public interest. The justification of an exclusive agreement must be assessed at least every three years. 'The purpose of this provision is to regulate a situation where a monopoly on the possession and release of public information has been transferred to a third party which is not itself a holder of information. The value that this provision seeks to protect is that recovery is open to all potential market players even if one market operator already offers added value products based on public information. The exclusive right can be interpreted here as the exclusive right to issue information in a reusable format. An exclusive agreement shall be concluded in a situation where the holder of the information does not transfer a public function but, for example, enters into an agreement to re-use public information and grants the holder of the re-use of the information the exclusive right to issue it. For example, the library can enter into a contract with a private person to digitize books in the library. Digitalised books would be easier to recycle to everyone in this way. Persons granted exclusive rights would be able to resell the re-usable information under normal conditions. This means that they have the right to provide a service and also to charge for it. Since such activities are not carried out within the framework of a public-law relationship, no conditions are laid down for them either. While there is a general prohibition on the granting of exclusive rights, public authorities may, in justified cases, consider it necessary to grant an exclusive right of re-use to a private party. Since there may also be other persons interested in holding and organising information, Directive 2003/98/EC prohibits the transfer of the exclusive right for more than three years, after which there is an obligation to justify the transfer of the exclusive right and to review the decision. One more minute, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U305	One minute more time.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U306	Thank you very much. Any more calls? There's no more calls. I'm closing the negotiations. There are four amendments to this draft. We'll go through them. Amendment 1 Please, Kadri Simson!

Peep Aru, member of the Constitutional Committee of the Riigikogu, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U385	Dear Vice-Chairman, Ladies and gentlemen, I thank you for the opportunity to perform here in front of you after waiting all night. We discussed today's item on the agenda with our Commission on 13 November. We also had specialists from the Ministry of Justice, the Ministry of Social Affairs and the Ministry of Finance. No amendments to the draft were received on time, but the Committee on Constitutional Affairs, together with the aforementioned ministries, made an amendment concerning the salaries and pensions of judges. Consequently, § 3 of the draft amending the Courts Act has been supplemented and the amendment of the Courts Act has also been included in the title. In short, the substance of the matter is that the salaries of judges will rise from 1 July 2013 to the levels laid down in the Law on salaries of senior civil servants. In contrast, today's form of personal pensions disappears, so that all those judges who take up their duties after 1 July 2013 receive the pension in the normal manner. This means that they can make their pension insurance just as most people in Estonia do. These judges, who have worked as judges and are already retired, will retain their pension as at 1 July 2013 and will be indexed each year to the index set out in the Law on salaries of senior civil servants. For those judges who have earned or are currently working and earning their retirement, the pension is calculated on the basis of the salary of the judge of the relevant level and will also be indexed each year by the coefficient given in the Law on salaries of senior civil servants. This is actually analogous to what is currently the case for members of the Riigikogu, the Chancellor of Justice and the Auditor General, and I hope that this pension reform will continue in Estonia. As a result of the Commission's discussion, we also eliminated one of the injustices of the Courts Act for a very long time. In particular, we have amended § 78 (4) of the current Judicial Act, which reduces the retirement age requirement of the President of the Supreme Court and stipulates that the right to receive a pension of 75% of the salary is reserved for the President of the Supreme Court who has worked in this capacity for at least five years, i.e. more than half of the current term of office of the President of the Supreme Court, which is nine years. So far, seven years of work were needed to achieve a 75% pension level. Since such an amendment concerns a respected former President of the Supreme Court, the current Chairman of the Constitutional Committee, a discussion was held without him. He opposed this decision, but the Commission maintained that such injustices had to be eliminated. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U414	The first reading of Bill 310 of Amendment to the Environmental Charges Act initiated by the Government of the Republic begins. I would like a presentation by Minister of Finance Jürgen Ligi of the speaker's desk!
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U415	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, In agreement with the municipalities, the municipal budgets will receive 25% of the mining right fee for mineral deposits of national importance and the associated water-specific use fee for 2011. The money released will be channelled to the municipal fine-tuning fund, with a four-year transitional compensatory allowance for adaptation to the amendment. The income base of the municipalities as a whole does not change. Municipalities with large mines are currently heavily dependent on environmental charges. With this change, we will provide them with a more stable income base, even if fees fall unexpectedly, as has happened in the case of one municipality. The amendment will help to harmonise the revenue base between municipalities. The environmental fee due to the municipalities helps to compensate for the suffering of mining. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U483	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, This draft was initiated on 5 November by the Government of the Republic and the Executive Board of the Riigikogu as the leading committee for processing the draft. The draft amends the allocation of the mining right fee and the associated specific water exploitation right fee from mineral deposits of national importance. The quality and quantity of mineral resources in such deposits are essential for the economic development of the country and the environmental impact of mining is wider than that of the local authority in which the deposits are located. The aim of the amendment is to harmonise the possibilities for local authorities to improve their living environment with fees. The draft was discussed in the Committee on the Environment on 12 November. The contents of the draft had been invited to be presented by the Head of the Department of Financial Management of Local Authorities of the Ministry of Finance, Sulev Liivik, who gave an overview of the main changes and impacts. The Environment Committee's questions were answered by Sulev Liivik and the Specialist of Local Government Financial Management at the Ministry of Finance, Pirgit Lohk. At its meeting on 12 November, the Commission decided by consensus to propose the completion of the first reading of bill 310. At its meeting on 22 November, the Committee on the Environment decided to propose to the President of the Riigikogu that the deadline for submitting amendments be set at 10.30 a.m. on 3 December. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U509	Madam President! Ladies and gentlemen, The nature of Estonia is our national wealth, and its benevolent use towards the future is primarily a matter of our mind, conscience and the way of life that results from it, only then an action aimed at the implementation of Estonian law, in particular the Constitutional provisions. That is why I fully justify the purposeful valuing of our natural resources by increasing the remuneration for their right of use over a longer period of time through transparent, publicised and dialogue with the people. Not in the way we see it, the Government of the Republic did on October 12th. a Regulation, which entered into force on 1 April 2013, increases many environmental charges by a quarter compared to the current one. This act was carried out secretly behind closed doors and suddenly, without prior disclosure of data in the information system and without involving interest groups in the discussion. Such an arrogant attitude, however, is wholly inherent in the government of its people, and so should this regulation of environmental charges be on its soul. This is a characteristic feature of our government that the right thing is being broken by adding honey cans. The draft law amending the Environmental Charges Act 310, which has been drafted by the Ministry of Finance and approved by the Government, is another example of how the current authorities use the legislator to do what they do. While, until now, according to § 13 (1) (2) of the Environmental Charges Act, 50% of the mineral mining right fee was received in the municipal budget of the mining site, only 25% will be received in the future. At the top level, the message in the explanatory memorandum states that the aim of the change is to harmonise the opportunities for local authorities to improve their living environment with fees. Mining fee is a very important source of income for municipalities. We know very well whose territory there are mines and careers. The explanatory memorandum to the draft law states that the collection of environmental charges does not involve the same statutory tasks for the municipality, since, according to Chapter 4 of the Groundbreaking Act, the municipality is not obliged to participate in the remediation of the land damaged by mining. Apparently, the government does not believe that, unlike them, local government leaders think of their own people. The importance of local government increases considerably in difficult times. It is the municipality that the person turns to first with his or her concerns and from where he or she hopes to receive help. Unfortunately, the government has forgotten this and, during the economic crisis, it has been the municipalities whose revenues have been cut most. The second additional budget for 2009 reduced the proportion of income tax paid by individuals to local governments from 11.9% to 11.4%. On the whole, it is only a few percentage points reduction, but it is actually very large sums. Moreover, this decision was made in the middle of the financial year, when the municipalities had signed contracts for the joint works of good order and the investment volumes put in place. Believe me, the stabilisation support for the local government revenue base from the Equalisation Fund, which is largely dependent on political pull winds, is not the solution. Knowing the extremely superior attitude of the government towards local governments, I can safely tell all those local authorities that are beyond mineral extraction areas that it is very difficult to believe the government's promises. And I mean, with this bill, the municipalities are losing the financial volumes they're supposed to get for the big job they're doing in their territories. I mean, the extra redistributive charge is all for itself, I mean, the government, it's still missing. The legislative amendment is fully counterproductive to one of the fundamental principles of the European Union's use of nature, i.e. the principle of subsidiarity. This also means using the compensation mechanism for environmental damage, precisely where the damage has been caused. Local authorities are currently protected from this directly from government maladministration. In the past, while it was clearly stated that the government must compensate financially for its negative activities vis-à-vis the local authorities, the government can now simply reduce the responsibilities of the municipalities to this extent. Let's just say that the law reduces the income of local governments and allows the municipality to shut down the community building to compensate for this, and it is no longer the responsibility of local governments to provide recreational activities. However, no one else deals with the provision of recreational activities on behalf of the municipalities. The decision of the Supreme Court of 16 March 2010 shows that legislation that imposes additional public obligations on local governments without funding from the state budget is incompatible with the Constitution. Now everything is legally correct; instead of simply financing additional commitments, existing commitments will be reduced. Local authorities maintain a link between people and public authorities. I mean, that's what we all know. However, government departments are trying to destroy this bridge in every way. I really don't understand what the local governments have done with this kind of anger. On behalf of the Centre Party Group, I propose that this draft be rejected. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U514	We begin the first reading of the draft law amending the Land Tax Act and the Income Tax Act initiated by the Government of the Republic. Minister of Finance Jürgen Ligi for the presentation.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U515	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Honourable Members of the Riigikogu! With this draft, we are trying to serve two objectives: firstly, to compensate local authorities for the reduction in the income tax on land by increasing the share of income tax they receive as a result of the tax exemption on the land under their residence. Another important change is the exemption from land tax on land occupied by state-owned public buildings. From 2013, the share of income tax received by municipalities will rise by 0.17 percentage points to 11,57% and from 2014 to 11,60%. As a result, municipal revenues will increase by EUR 10.5 million in 2013, with a total impact of EUR 13.3 million in 2014. The revenue from the increase in the income tax rate covers the overall reduction in the income from the land tax. Municipalities with a lower-than-average income base will be balanced against the impact of the changes through the Equalisation Fund. The law is intended to enter into force in two stages, as the Ministry of Finance estimates that the impact of the land tax exemption on local governments' receipts is increasing. In order to benefit from the benefit, people will optimize the tax, for example by registering their place of residence. The land tax exemption is granted on land owned by state-owned public buildings. In most countries of the European Union, state-owned real estate does not pay taxes to municipalities. The change will have the effect of reducing the revenue of local governments by 0.3 million euros. The increase in the share of income tax covers the reduction in the income of the land tax. Local government units calculate land tax exemptions and concessions differently, therefore it is necessary to specify the procedure for calculating the exemption from the land tax. For example, if the boundary of the densely populated area or the separation line of the settlement unit passes through the cadastral unit, the extent of the land tax exemption shall be determined on the basis of which the majority of the area of the cadastral unit belongs. Thank you!

ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U638	We will go to the next item, which is the first reading of the Act amending the Law on the Financial Management of a Local Government Unit initiated by the Government of the Republic and the Law on the Organisation of Local Government Act 311. I ask for a presentation of the speech chair by Secretary of Finance Jürgen Ligi!
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U639	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Honourable Members of the Riigikogu! The purpose of this draft is to contribute to improving the financial management of local authorities. In 2012, the Law on the Financial Management of the Local Government Unit entered into force and, in the course of its implementation, the Ministry of Finance has been able to improve its proposals. The Council is responsible for the good financial performance of the municipality. The role of the council in strategic decision-making and the use of taxpayers' money increases with ownership. Upon the entry into force of the Act, only with the permission of the Council may enter into cost-creating contracts, take long-term commitments and grant loans to entities dependent on the municipality. The ability of municipalities to react to income losses becomes more flexible. For example, the results of the core activity can be planned as negative in one year, and it is important that a balance is ensured during the budgetary strategy period. In the coming years, the budget will have to be planned with a surplus. It is important to learn lessons from the economic crisis in Europe from the financial management of municipalities. The high indebtedness of municipalities increases the risk of financial difficulties and hampers development in a distant perspective. Rapid growth in government debt also negatively affects government budgetary positions and forces other taxpayers to save. According to the amendment, until the end of 2016, the net debt burden on municipalities must not exceed 60% of the income of the core business, nor must new debts be incurred. It is certainly reasonable to limit the debt assumption of a municipality with a high debt burden. An analysis of previous years shows that the indebtedness of local governments has had a negative impact on the government budget. According to our forecast, the budgetary position limit will help to improve this position by EUR 67 million in four years. Thank you.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U640	Thank you very much. We'll get to the questions. Please, Marika Tuus-Laul!

ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U751	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, When I introduce this speech, I must thank the Chairman of the Central Party's Municipal Council, Jüri Ratas, who is one of the not very many members of the Riigikogu who have passed through all the local governments of Estonia and met the elders and mayors. Thanks to these direct contacts, he also knows what concerns the laws adopted in the Riigikogu can cause to local governments in their daily lives. The draft law amending the Financial Management Act and the Local Government Organisation Act extends the current limit on the increase in net debt from 2012 in accordance with the principles set out in the State Budget Strategy 2013-2016. The purpose of the extension of the restriction is to ensure the financial stability of local authorities in a volatile economic environment and to avoid potential financial risks. The fundamental error of the law is that it is recommended that the restriction be maintained for all municipalities, regardless of their economic capacity and future prospects. This hampers the development of a number of local authorities with the potential to carry out the necessary investments that would temporarily lead to a debt burden of more than 60%. Today it is the right day to recall that the Act on the financial management of the local government unit adopted by the Riigikogu in September 2010 decided that in the future, municipalities with a higher than average solvency may borrow up to 100% of the budget, but in autumn 2011, the government proposed to postpone the increase in the net debt burden of local governments by one year, i.e. until 2013. Now, one month before the beginning of 2013, we have before us a draft which, when adopted as a law, stipulates that the net indebtedness of any municipality must not rise by more than 60% of the operating revenue until at least the end of 2016. The reason for this decision is strange. The explanatory memorandum to the draft law states that the aim of the amendments is to ensure the financial stability of local authorities in a volatile economic environment, to avoid possible financial risks and to improve the fiscal position of the general government. I cannot understand how the Government of the Republic can at one point talk about Estonia's rapid economic growth and welfare-enhancing national budgets and at the same time make statements that raise doubts as to whether the economic crisis is over or just ahead of us. It is also sad that the government still makes weighty and long-term decisions on its own and does not take into account the views of representatives of local governments. For example, the Managing Director of the Estonian Association of Rural Governments said that in the negotiations between local governments and government on the 2013 state budget, both sides wanted the loan limit to end this year. Probably later, it was decided differently in the back room somewhere. The basic logic states that it is important to find a compromise that is satisfactory for all parties to the various positions. In the case of more economically able municipalities, the maximum permissible debt burden could increase gradually and initially by at least 10 to 20%. Unfortunately, the Government of the Republic does not speak the language of compromises, and today the only argument in this Chamber is the use of a stronger right. How must municipalities who find it difficult to comply with the obligations imposed by law act? For example, they may be in a situation where additional investments are needed to increase the number of school or kindergarten places, but the necessary loans may not be taken. It is high time the government coalition understood that local authority is the closest to man and therefore the most immediately perceived. Due to the recent economic crisis and the politics of the Government of the Republic, most local authorities are difficult not to say, to fulfil all the obligations imposed on them by law. Any additional restrictions will further aggravate their situation. The reform party and the IRL government are approaching the financial situation of local governments from a completely wrong perspective. A political decision was taken that the debt burden of local governments should not increase because it is feared that local governments will not be able to serve their debt commitments.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U752	Would you like some extra time?
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U753	At the same time, local government revenues were repeatedly cut in recent years, which clearly makes it difficult for local governments to fulfil their obligations. More specifically, two local taxes were abolished, the income tax allocated to local authorities was reduced, and the loss of income resulting from the tax exemption of the land under home is not covered by other receipts despite the promises. The decision taken in 2009 to adopt the second supplementary budget, which reduced the proportion of income tax paid by individuals to local governments from 11,93% to 11,4%, meant the withdrawal of hundreds of millions of kroons from the municipal budget. In addition, the decision was taken in the middle of the financial year, which means that the municipalities had already concluded a number of contracts for the maintenance and construction works and put in place the necessary investments. The law exempting homeowners from land tax was adopted in summer 2011. It is absurd that the state is not fulfilling its promise to compensate local authorities for the loss of land tax entirely from the state budget. According to the Government Office's decision, the land tax exemption is to be compensated by an increase in the share of income tax received by municipalities to 11,6% by 2014. Elementary mathematics says that the share of income tax received by municipalities must first be raised back to 11.93%, and only above that figure will compensation for the tax exemption of the land under residence begin. The laws of the Republic of Estonia allow local governments to introduce six different taxes: advertising tax, road and street closure tax, motor vehicle tax, animal housing tax, amusement tax and parking fee. In the last five years, between 2007 and 2011, no municipality used motor vehicle tax or amusement tax. The animal and boat taxes were valid for 1 to 3 municipalities. It can therefore be said that local authorities have only three real local taxes, which represent only 1% of their fiscal tax revenues. According to the laws currently in force, municipalities have a lot of important obligations, but it is very difficult for them to improve their own finances. 99% of the municipality's right to decide on the tax revenue is in the hands of the central authority and the government coalition decides in the same way how much loans may be taken by local authorities. As is the case with the introduction of an equal debt limit, administrative reform is also intended to be carried out mechanically. In fact, the situation of each city and rural municipality must be looked at separately, as well as future assumptions and possible mergers. It is clear that the current administrative arrangements, including municipal funding, require clear and fundamental changes. We need a new system of local governments, which means both administrative reform and local tax reform. The law adopted at the moment, however, does not alleviate the problems faced by the Estonian municipalities; rather, it puts many more progressive local governments in a difficult situation. We find that the sooner the Government of the Republic starts to view local governments as the foundation of the functioning of its state and an equal partner, the better for Estonia. However, as long as this is not done, the Group of the Estonian Centre Party proposes to reject the draft law amending the Financial Management Act and the Local Government Organisation Act at first reading. Thank you for your attention.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U761	Mr President of the Riigikogu. Honourable Members of the Riigikogu! The draft proposed by this government aims to provide a legal basis for the implementation of the so-called "single window" electronic marine information system. The system will be used to provide documentation and other information on ships calling at Estonian ports and on-board cargo to the national authorities. The integrated data system will be connected to the Maritime Administration, Tax and Customs Board, Police and Border Guard Board, Veterinary and Food Board, Agricultural Board, Health Board, Environmental Information Centre, Environmental Inspectorate and Statistics Board, who will all have access to the necessary documents. The Maritime Information System will reduce the administrative burden for the vessel owner's representative, as the need for overlapping information to be sent to different target groups through different communication channels will be removed. The amendment would enter into force on 1 July 2013. The development of the marine information system is already taking place since 2011 and costs just over EUR 300000. The funds have been allocated by the Government of the Republic through the organisation of the Information Society Investment Plan on 1 July 2010 from the Structural Funds. ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U867	Mr President-in-Office of the Council. Ladies and gentlemen, As a formal approach, the purpose of this draft law is to bring maritime safety law and port law into line with the requirements of European Union legislation and international conventions and to simplify and harmonise the administrative procedures applicable to maritime transport, in particular on the basis of the requirements of Directive 2010/65/EU of the European Parliament and of the Council, which provides for the obligation for Member States to ensure that notification formalities are completed electronically and transmitted via a single interface. The result should be a significant reduction in the administrative burden on maritime transport and an increase in the attractiveness of maritime transport as a significant and environmentally friendly transport channel. In the explanatory memorandum, we read that the draft is not related to the programme of action of the Government of the Republic. I wonder why it is not in the government's programme of action. It seems to be a very noble thing to do, but, see, there is no program. From time to time, we still hear complaints about the bureaucracy of the euro. Sometimes we curse these directives and all kinds of precepts, but see, there are areas where pressure and precepts from the European Union help us move forward. One such area is certainly maritime. I have always been dismayed and inquisitive about why all the last successive governments and not only the previous governments, but the latter, in particular, have not wanted to understand in any way that maritime affairs are the most important industries in a country with a long sea border. It is as if Estonia has been tried to move from the status of a sea state to the status of a seaside state. While the icebreaking development plan has been adopted, it has simply not been completed. You can always find reasons not to. It's never a problem. There has also been a wish to eliminate maritime education and unexpectedly water charges were raised six times, so that ships would not come to our ports, so there would be one less concern, so icebreakers would not have to be obtained. Our maritime policy, or the maritime development plan, was finally prepared thanks to the demands of the European Union. The maritime communities of their country pressed for nearly 20 years to start developing it, but for the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications, it was like water on the goose's back. So, in some cases, it is very good that the structures of the European Union are like a sacred cow to our government. One cough, and the legs come out of the belly by themselves, and things start to move. The same is true of this bill. We learn from the explanatory memorandum that the authorities that collect information from persons operating by sea by law are more than enough: Maritime Administration, Police and Border Guard Board, Tax and Customs Board, Environmental Inspectorate, Environmental Information Centre, Agricultural Board, Veterinary and Food Board, Health Board, Statistics Board and certainly a few more. In addition, the law provides for the basis for the operation of a private company established by the State to provide pilotage services and the procedure for the transmission of information to it, as well as for the transmission of information to private ports. This list is certainly not exhaustive either. Many data are provided to public authorities in duplicate and using different channels. The authorities may require the information to be provided in paper form or by fax or e-mail or by entering it in the information systems. There are provisions of the law that oblige the same data to be entered electronically in the Estonian Tax and Customs Board's information system as well as in the Finnish Maritime Administration's information system. The question is, why is that? The answer is very simple: these information systems do not interact, so the same data must be entered twice. It's as simple as that. This is all happening in our own pompous and praised e-governance, where we cannot even communicate with the two information systems, but we very much want to engage in e-elections. Now, thanks to the instigation of EU authorities, we can process a draft law that provides a legal basis for a single electronic transmission of data and thus contributes to reducing the administrative burden on maritime transport. The law itself, as has been said, provides a legal basis. In order for this to work, an electronic integrated maritime information system must be established. In this respect, it is a pleasure to note that, in parallel with legislation, practical aspects have already been addressed and that EUR 318408 was planned to be invested in the creation of the system in 2011 and 2012. Of course, on the basis of the Structural Funds: you told me to develop, then give me the money. The financing of maintenance work is foreseen from the budget of the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications and it would be very good not to increase water charges again to cover these amounts. If the system works, it should be a big deal. A representative of the vessel owner, be it an agent or a captain, could enter all the data in a single window, and the authorities will pick out each one of their own. This time, by looking to the European Union, we can take a small step forward in the development of the maritime cluster again. The Estonian Centre Party Group supports the further processing of this draft. Thank you.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U868	No more calls, I'll close the negotiations. The commission has proposed that the first reading of the 300 draft be completed. I will set the deadline for the submission of 300 amendments on 14 December 2012 at 5 p.m. The first reading is closed and the fifth item is also closed.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U869	We are going to the sixth item, which is the first reading of the draft law amending the Electricity Market Act, initiated by the Government of the Republic. I would like to ask the Minister of Economic Affairs and Communications, Juhan Parts, to present the speech chair of the Riigikogu!
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U870	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Honourable members of the Riigikogu! The Government will submit a draft law amending the Electricity Market Act to the Riigikogu for approval, whose main aim is to amend the principle of subsidies for electricity produced from renewable sources and in efficient cogeneration in such a way as to minimise the burden on the consumer and also meet the criteria for authorisation of State aid laid down by the European Commission. There are three main problems with the current renewable energy support scheme. Firstly, there is no possibility of effectively regulating the increase in renewable electricity production, resulting in a disproportionate burden on the consumer. Secondly, the existing support for renewable electricity is fixed as an unchanged subsidy rate paid by the transmission system operator as a subsidy to the producer and the actual support is not linked to the market price for the sale of electricity. This ensures, on the one hand, an unduly high level of profitability, since regardless of the market price, the aid paid is unchanged. This also leads to the fact that this support is contrary to the European Union State Aid Guidelines and qualifies as prohibited State aid, overcompensation. And thirdly, the real need for support for producers varies. This depends, inter alia, on the size of the initial investment, the fuel used, the market price of electricity, the capacity of the production facilities. The current support scheme does not take into account the need for different support and provides for the same support for all renewable electricity generation installations. In addition to the disproportionate nature of the scheme, renewable electricity subsidies will become State aid in the light of the fully opening electricity market in 2013. According to § 301 (21) of the Competition Act, the grantor of State aid may not grant State aid without the authorisation of the European Commission. According to the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications and the Ministry of Finance, the support for renewable energy from 2013 onwards constitutes State aid and the European Commission may suspend the payment of subsidies. We are proposing new subsidy principles. In summary, these are the following: Firstly, a lower limit is set for the renewable electricity to be supported. The amount supported will be dependent on the overall renewable energy target taken by the Estonian state, our energy portfolio. Secondly, in the event of a shortfall in production volume due to the objectives, the most favourable producer for the consumer will be supported. Thirdly, the aid depends on the market price of electricity and carbon quotas. The aid will be differentiated on an ad hoc basis, in other words, as far as possible, given the different production capacities and the different sources of renewable energy, the possible rates of aid will be differentiated in order to avoid overcompensation. The support scheme proposed in the draft is a compromise solution, but despite the investments already made under the law adopted by the Riigikogu in 2007, it will save consumers around EUR 300 million for renewable electricity by 2020. If we did not change this law and continue to do so for the next seven years, consumers would pay EUR 300 million more. That's a considerable amount. In our opinion, the compromise that has been negotiated also creates the preconditions for obtaining authorisation for State aid. With the amendment of the scheme and the entry into force of the law, the renewable energy charge is estimated to be 18% lower than it is today. There was also news today that next year's renewable energy fee is already falling. It may be said that the impact of this draft has already been felt, but if it is adopted as a law, then the system operator Elering will make the corresponding calculations. If we are not able to adopt this law this year because of a constructive discussion in this Chamber, we will have to provide the necessary implementing powers between the first and second readings so that the recalculation of the renewable energy fee can also be made in the middle of the year. So far, we were hoping that it would be possible to run on a calendar year-by-year basis. The amount supported is dependent on the implementation plan of the Renewable Energy Action Plan, this is the Estonian National Renewable Energy Action Plan approved by the Government of the Republic, which reflects the share of electricity produced from renewable sources in consumption. If electricity produced with the support of investments already made is not sufficient to meet the objectives set out in the Renewable Energy Action Plan implementation plan in some years to come, the Government of the Republic may, on the basis of an analysis by the Ministry of Economic Affairs, appoint a producer with its own organisation, who produces the missing part at the lowest cost and is paid for it. Why should the law enter into force this year? It's a big question! The law must be passed this year for two reasons. Firstly, the grants must comply with the State aid criteria, and then they can continue to be paid out. Secondly, renewable energy charges for consumers must be reduced from the beginning of next year, which will take place in an unavoidable parallel context with the opening of the electricity market; this would certainly be compensatory in view of the possible increase in electricity prices on the basis of market prices. The adoption of the Law amending the Electricity Market Act this year is also necessary in order to bring the aid scheme for the following year into line with the conditions of the State aid permit. The scheme currently in force is not in line with these requirements because it is not linked to the market price of electricity and therefore allows producers to make unduly high profits. The adoption of the draft will also depend in time on the speed with which the European Commission grants State aid authorisation. Until the law is passed, we cannot start formal procedures for the application for authorisation of State aid in the European Commission. According to § 301 (21) of the Competition Act, State aid may not be granted without authorisation and according to the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications and the Ministry of Finance, the current support for renewable energy constitutes State aid and the Commission may terminate its payment. The Ministry of Economic Affairs has started asking for authorisation of state aid according to the scheme set out in the draft, but I repeat: the deadlines will run once the law has been passed. Finally, so much has been agreed in the government: as regards the subsidies paid to the 11th energy block of the Baltic Power Plant of Eesti Energia Narva Power Plant, according to the Government's decision and its draft, the energy block will not receive support for electricity produced from renewable energy from 1 January 2013. This does not in itself mean that AS Narva Power Plants could not use biomass if it was economically viable but could not count on its support. If the payment of the aid continues, this will also mean imposing an additional burden on the consumer. The subsidies would be financed by a consumer tariff and any increase in electricity prices next year would be a great additional burden for the consumer. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U988	Thank you very much, Mr Jaan Apple Tree. I would now like to ask my colleague, Toomas Tõniste, as a representative of the Group of the Pro Patria and Res Publica Alliance.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U989	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, Today's draft aims to reduce the distortions of competition caused by the scheme for supporting renewable energy sources and to reduce the unduly high economic burden on consumers. In order for this to happen, we need to thoroughly examine some of the issues that raised questions. Firstly, the draft does not establish a clear financial limit for subsidies, with the exception of wind energy to which it already applies. The draft includes a back door through which existing producers will receive support for all electricity produced in the future, with a much higher output than the 915 gigawatt-hours charged in 2013. In addition, existing producers can apply to the Competition Authority for a higher amount of aid fixed by law to ensure a yield of at least 10%. However, those whose productivity is currently considerably higher are not affected by this law. Another factor to be added is to compensate for the rise in the price of biomass. Secondly, I want to stop at manufacturers using the wind as an energy source. In 2013, there will be a limitation on support for wind farms as their total production exceeds 600 gigawatt-hours. According to the calculations, effective support will decrease to 83% in 2014 and to 76% in 2015, a total of 24%. The question even arises as to why it is necessary to change the current system at all if there is a clear cost limit and there is nothing more from the state. And why is it necessary to draw a line backwards so that new companies, which have already made significant expenditures, are left at the door? In conclusion, I would like to say that this is a good draft, which actually reduces consumers' electricity bills. We have an opportunity to improve this bill together and to adopt it as soon as possible. Thank you.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U990	Thank you very much, Mr Tõniste. I would now like to ask for the floor as a representative of the Group of the Centre Party of Kadri Simson.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U991	Dear Chairman of the Riigikogu! Ladies and gentlemen, I will continue where Mr Tõniste concluded his speech: from the confirmation that the draft will actually help to reduce consumers' electricity bills. Indeed, this is the case in the form of a reduction in the renewable energy charge of 18% really has an impact on electricity prices. At present, the household consumer has to pay 11.71 euro cents for one kilowatt-hour of electricity. On 1 January, we will be faced with opening up the electricity market, which is to buy electricity on the stock market. Plus Elering has been granted permission to increase the network fee by 13.3%. The household consumer should therefore pay 13,97 cents instead of 11,71 cents per kilowatt hour from January. But if the draft we are discussing today is adopted as proposed by the Minister for Economic Affairs, the price increase would be from 11.71 cents to 13.77 cents, or a good 0.2 cent win. This is a necessary step, but not enough. Its impact is two to three times less than the one proposed by the Centre Party to reduce electricity excise duty to the minimum permitted in the European Union. Of course, we come to the aid of the Minister for Economic Affairs in his attempt to reduce home expenses, but this effort is not sincere. Government and government departments have far more effective levers to influence the rise in electricity prices. The price of electricity consists of five parts and four of them are clearly influenced by political decisions. In addition to the payment for renewable energy, the political decision is also the amount of electricity excise duty in Estonia. So far, the decision of the Government and the Minister for Economic Affairs has been that it is 4.5 times higher than the minimum rate for the European Union. During this period, the Minister for Economic Affairs also increased the value added tax (VAT) on electricity. All these taxes have hit hard on people who are already hard to deal with. Praxis, a research company, has done an analysis of how the price of electricity affects different households in Estonia and has divided residents into five income segments. The richest, who certainly includes the Minister for Economic Affairs, do not pay much attention to the price of electricity, because in theory they pay only EUR 3 for electricity out of every 100 euros they spend. However, the poorest fifth of every 100 euros they hold must give EUR 9 to pay for electricity. This slope obviously hits them a lot more. Lowering the electrical excise would help these people the most. I am not talking about the fact that rising electricity prices not only have a direct impact on home-related costs, but also lead to higher prices in all other areas of life. Therefore, the party that signed a contract with the electorate before the elections and made a promise that it would stand for lower domestic costs should not be limited to a reduction in the symbolic renewable energy fee, which has too little impact. It is also noteworthy that in a situation where the Minister of Economic Affairs is obviously stuck in negotiations with his coalition partner, he still comes before the Riigikogu. I would point out that this accusation was not correct. Edgar Savisaar was the Minister of Economic Affairs at a time when the Riigikogu dared to take decisions that were somewhat different from the drafts received from the government. The drafts submitted by Edgar Savisaare and the Ministry of Economic Affairs did not have the same face as the Riigikogu adopted them. Perhaps the Riigikogu used too much of its power, but at least at that time it was the Riigikogu that did not vote through the drafts coming from the government without making a critical analysis and without having the courage of such an analysis. Three more minutes, please.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U1085	Dear Chairman, Ladies and gentlemen, On 7 November, the Group of the Estonian Reform Party and the Group of the Isamaa and Res Publica Association initiated a draft amendment of § 177 (8) of the Employment Contracts Act. The draft was appointed by the Riigikogu Board as the commission of the Social Affairs Committee, who discussed the draft before the first reading at three sessions: 12 November, 22 November and 30 November. On 22 November, the Social Affairs Committee invited the Chairman of the Estonian Trade Union Confederation, the Chairman of the Management Board of TALO and the representative of the Estonian Employers' Confederation. On 30 November, or today, at the extraordinary session of the Social Affairs Committee, a new deadline for submitting amendments was set, due to the organisation of the Riigikogu's work, i.e. the fact that the first reading will take place today. The Government of the Republic supported this draft at its meeting on 15 November. The Committee on Social Affairs decided to present the draft as amended at first reading. The changes are technical, not substantive. This draft, as amended, amends the title and deletes the reference to a specific point of law. According to § 35 (3) of the Rules on Standard Techniques of Drafts to be dealt with in the Riigikogu, the title of the draft Act amending the Act does not indicate the structural elements to be amended. Compared to the original draft, the publication of the Employment Contracts Act has changed. In the case of an amendment to an unenforced amendment, the publication of the law containing the amendment provision, in this case the publication of the original text of the Employment Contracts Act, shall be given as a publication mark: RT I, 2009, 5, 35. For reasons of legal clarity, the text of § 1 of the draft has been specified and reference has been made to the law which is essentially amended and to that provision. At its meeting on 12 November, the Committee on Social Affairs decided to send a draft to 320 plenary sessions as amended and to propose that the first reading be closed. The Committee on Social Affairs proposes that the deadline for submitting amendments be set at 12 noon on 3 December. Thank you.

Does anyone in this hall dare to claim that there are mothers in Estonia who prefer to raise a child in half starvation and deep poverty from laziness? Since this is a direct incitement to tension and animosity in society, the government seems to be of little interest,' said Harri Tali, Chairman of the Confederation of Trade Unions, in his press release. In other words, it means that the government forces the most vulnerable group in society to pay the unemployed medical staff. Unfortunately, we have no choice but to accept this harsh assessment. Not only is it necessary to include patients, including children. Today, the next increase in bed pay was discussed at Tallinn Children's Hospital and their hands expanded. The consequence is, in any case, that, as if in fulfilling the promise given to the medical staff, the government coalition continues its well-known policy of division and government, playing one interest group against another, while not even the introduction of reserves or dividends by the health insurance fund has been discussed. In front of our eyes, there is nothing more than an impertinent redistribution of poverty, in a way that divides society and deepens social contradictions. Therefore, the Estonian Centre Party Group cannot support the draft and proposes to reject it at first reading.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U1128	Thank you. No more calls, I'll finish the negotiations. Since two political groups, the Estonian Centre Party Group and the Social Democratic Party Group, have proposed to reject the bill 320 at first reading, we must vote it through. Please, colleague Mailis Reps!

ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U1135	Thank you. With bill 268 we have decided to take an important step towards what alcohol producers wanted: we share the increase in alcohol excise duty over several years. Now, however, a legitimate question arises. A common person who faces a very significant increase in the price of electricity that will affect his life from 1 January does not realise that if he met the alcohol producers, why can't he delay the increase in the price of electricity to, for example, three, five or seven years, why he has to start paying for this very big increase from 1 January? As a result, people have a legitimate concern. If we look at the much-accepted 2013 budget, we will see that some sectors are allowed to have 4.7% wage increases, but the likely inflation resulting from the price of electricity will eat up all this increase. So, despite the very high level of promises, the real situation of people next year will not be eased, even in a situation where perhaps there is hope for wage increases in some sectors. Today, the Chairman of the Social Affairs Committee stressed on one of the previously discussed drafts that the 5% increase in pensions and the need-based child benefit of EUR 9 59 cents will alleviate people's situation. Unfortunately, however, we missed the fact that people are already aware today of how much their electricity bills are likely to be higher next year. However, people's concerns are much wider, as electricity prices in turn affect a large number of sectors. This also affects small businesses, which are already much less competitive in rural areas, as demonstrated by the survey of the University of Rural Sciences. A summary of the possibilities and situation of the manufacturing industry was published today by SEB's analyst. He also pointed out that there was no hope or great optimism. Why is that? It is precisely because the sadness and hopelessness caused by the cuts made during the crisis has lasted for so many years, and there is a great deal of apathy and giving up. What do people expect of the Riigikogu? They want to know what these two, three or ten steps are to give people back optimism and no chance of surviving these difficult times if the pay does not rise as quickly as inflation increases, if pensions do not rise to such an extent as to allow a single-living pensioner to live normally, and if long- promised needs-based child support does not support families in deep poverty enough to actually get them out of the poverty cycle. Why does electricity affect all of us? Of course, you can respond optimisticly, as the Prime Minister said, that if you cannot consume electricity, the lamp must be turned off. Unfortunately, there are families where such extreme measures have been taken because of very limited opportunities. However, I hope that we all understand that under Nordic conditions there are costs that cannot be brought close to zero, and one of them is the price paid for electricity. If we look at our options next year, a reduction in the renewable energy fee (which represents just over 1% of the electricity bill) proposed by the Minister for Economic Affairs and already in the process will, of course, not lead to a real fall in prices for people. It is certainly important to use a number of measures together. It would certainly be wise for coalition partners to look seriously again at our proposal to minimise the amount of electricity excise duty allowed in the European Union, from EUR 4.5 to EUR 1, or to provide themselves with serious measures that would make the increase in electricity prices easier for people on 1 January. I come back to the third reading of Bill 268. There have been no such negotiations. It is certainly not right to tell us today that everything has been done, whether at European or Estonian level, so that the price increase next year will not be the most painful hit of those people who are already struggling. Are serious changes to the state budget made before the third reading? We hope so. The Estonian person is modest, but he is also quite active outside the Riigikogu. I hope that the members of the Riigikogu have also been able to visit nature and people during the intermission of the sessions. At least those who have visited Viru County, but certainly in rural areas, maybe outside Tallinn, have heard how people want something practical to be offered here. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U1141	Dear Vice-Chairman of the Riigikogu, Happy Members of the Riigikogu! At third reading, there is a draft law amending the State Fees Act, initiated by the Government of the Republic. Draft Act amending the Law on state fees...
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U1142	Sorry, waitress! Ladies and gentlemen, please be a little quieter. Please, Priit Tobal!
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U1143	Thank you. The development of the draft law amending the State Fees Act was made necessary by the need to update the state fee for driving licences and identity documents issued in the State Fees Act and for documents issued under the consular service in such a way as to cover the costs of issuing or issuing the document. According to the explanatory memorandum to the draft law, the purpose of the law is to adjust the rates of state fees for some documents issued or issued by a foreign agency. § 1 of the Bill amends and rewords § 207 of the Tax Act. A national fee of EUR 25,56 shall be paid for the issue or exchange of the first driving licence, driving licence, international driving licence or duplicate of a motor vehicle plus an additional EUR 30 for the issue in a foreign agency, which shall cover the additional costs incurred by the foreign agency. In total, therefore, a state fee of EUR 55,56 is paid for the issue of the first driving licence, driving licence, international driving licence or duplicate of a motor vehicle at a diplomatic mission. The draft also amends paragraphs 1, 7 and 9 of § 2572 of the State Fees Act and adds new paragraphs 15 and 16. The state fee paid for the examination of the application for the issue of an identity document in the foreign representation provided for in § 2572 of the State Fees Act shall be increased in such a way that the applicable rates of state fees cover the costs of issuing an identity document. In this context, the draft also draws attention to the fact that, if the requested document has already been forwarded by the Police and Border Guard Board to the Estonian diplomatic mission or honorary consul as indicated in the application form by the applicant, but the applicant decides to change the issue of the document, additional costs will be incurred in changing the place of issue. The draft therefore introduces an additional rate of state fees to regulate the situation if the person wishes to change the place where the identity document is issued. Point 6 of the draft act relates to the fact that it is costly to issue a document through a foreign representation of a Member State of the European Union and that it has been paid up to now by the Ministry of Foreign Affairs out of its operating costs. In the future, the applicant for the document or the change in the place where the document is issued will have to pay his or her expenses. For example, the cost of sending a passport issued by a foreign mission of a Member State of the European Union may amount to up to EUR 90. The Ministry of Foreign Affairs has also added the state fees of other Member States for examining the application for the issue of an identity document at a foreign agency to compare the explanatory memorandum. For example, in Austria, there is EUR 79.50 to be paid inside the country, in the Finnish diplomatic mission 75.50 plus 70 euros, in Portugal the national fee is EUR 60, in the Finnish diplomatic mission 75 euros, in Sweden the national fee is EUR 45, in the Finnish diplomatic mission 157 euros, in Finland the national fee is EUR 54 and in the Finnish diplomatic mission 120 euros, in the UK the national fee is EUR 95 and in the Finnish diplomatic mission 195 euros. Last but not least, the Czech Republic has a national fee of EUR 25 and a fee of EUR 50 in the Finnish diplomatic mission. Can I have three more minutes?
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U1144	Three minutes extra time. In the meantime, ladies and gentlemen, those who do not care about Priit Toobal's speech may negotiate on the other side of the door. Please, Priit Tobal, let's continue!
