To a certain time, a person must be able to meet these physical requirements. If he can't do it, he's gonna have to leave this job and find another job. This has led to prison officials leaving for other institutions, either the police, the police, or somewhere else. This is perfectly natural and normal in the area of internal security. But obviously, the loss of physical strength associated with human natural ageing is not the only reason for the staff turnover of the prison department. Unfortunately, we do not have information and there are no studies on why prison workers are very often changing. Large and expensive recruitment campaigns have also been unsuccessful. Alongside the limited training of the prison authorities, there is a problem that does not favour the objectives of imprisonment, including excessively large, so-called industrial prisons. Such large prisons are, in fact, major exceptions in developed countries. We remember how the former Mayor of Tartu, Andrus Ansip, eagerly fought against the plan of his party partner, the Minister of Justice, to accommodate 1,000 prisoners in Tartu instead of the promised and planned 500 prisoners. Finnish newspapers say that the Helsinki Central Prison, which has a maximum of 300 inmates, is too large. So what about Tartu Prison? Modernising the organisation of prisons is a very big and serious job. These details, which are written in the draft law being processed, are just a few crumbs, so there is actually much to hope that they will result in a qualitative change. Thank you.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U1232	Thank you very much. I'm asking for a speech chair at Marika Tuus-Laul! Eight minutes.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U1233	Good chairman of the sitting! Ladies and gentlemen, First of all, as an introduction and for the record, 2600 convicted persons are serving sentences in Estonian prisons as of April. In addition, 762 arrests are in prison. Thus, the special regime applies to more than 3000 persons, which is approximately equal to the population of the city of Tõrva. It is a very large group of persons whose fundamental rights are limited. When it comes to the general principles of punishment, imprisonment, as a form of punishment, cannot be an end in itself, but must still be an aim of the return of people to society as persons of full value. Speaking of this bill, it has to be said that more attention will be paid in the future to banning in the case of prisoners. For example, it sets out the grounds for prohibiting short and long-term meetings and makes the regulation of both personal and prohibited property of detainees more precise. The law includes specific grounds for which the injured person has the right to information. This right applies only to major offences. It doesn't make any sense why the victims are being distinguished. On the basis of the principle of equal treatment, this information should, as it were, be provided by all the victims, not only by those whose rights have been seriously violated, as stated in the explanatory memorandum. The unequal treatment of people in similar situations may seem to be discrimination. Paragraph 15 of the Prisons Act is amended. Its paragraph 2 specifies the regulation of prohibited items. In certain circumstances, objects which are not in conformity with the purposes of imprisonment, such as video games, television, etc., shall be regarded as prohibited articles. If a person has been sentenced to imprisonment for murder and this prisoner wishes to play a game with a game console at night and day, then such a restriction is really understandable. However, does this too general wording not disproportionately affect the rights of other detainees? On a positive note, if the inmate or the arrested person communicates with his lawyer or his representative, the bill will no longer allow their conversation to be eavesdropped or the letters read. The choice of the customer's protection tactics is confidential information which may not be obtained in violation of the rights of the defence. The state prosecutor's office has confirmed in the media that guaranteeing the right of defence is a duty of the rule of law and that eavesdropping of a client and a lawyer is illegal. It's been an old law gap. It is a good thing that it has now been eliminated, but it is a pity that it has been done relatively late. One change has been introduced by the Ministry of the Interior, which also includes the Defence Police. They have proposed to include in the draft the possibility of restricting, in justified cases, external and internal communication by a prisoner convicted of an offence against the state. While the explanatory memorandum has analysed the limitation of communication with non-village persons, the in-village communication has not yet been fully explained here. As I have already said, imprisonment, as a form of punishment, cannot be an end in itself, but must still be an aim of the return of a decent person to society. Thus, a prison officer, who has to comply with the law and comply with it correctly, has a great responsibility. I will give you an example from the explanatory memorandum. If, after being seated in the brig, the prisoner has left a button open, the official has the right to punish him, for example, by prohibiting the use of a water heating device for up to 45 days. Where is the limit that a prison officer will not exercise unlimited power here? Paragraph 63 (1) establishes the type of disciplinary sanction, which is to prohibit the use of personal radio, television or other electrical equipment required for up to 45 days. According to the bill, this type of punishment can be imposed after the most serious disciplinary action, which is to sit in jail. You're gonna carry that punishment, and then you're gonna end it with another, a little less severe punishment, such as a ban on the use of an electric shaving machine. However, according to good practice of punishment, a lower penalty is usually applied at first and, if this does not work, a more stringent penalty is applied. But it's the other way around. It may not be approved by the European Court of Human Rights either. Prisoners can sue their cartel, for example. Another amendment concerning the challenge of an administrative act. We know that a challenge to an administrative act must be guaranteed if a person considers that his or her rights have been infringed. However, the amendment seeks to bring the review and resolution of the dispute to a lower level than has been the case so far. In my opinion, there is a risk that if the appeal is resolved at a lower level than the head of the administration, this could lead to a dissipation of responsibility. I believe that both the issuing of an administrative act and the review of the appeal should, however, take place at the same level. I would also like to say that we must ensure that the purpose of the execution of imprisonment is met and, at the same time, that the fundamental rights of the people are not violated. However, if they are affected, it must be ensured that as little as possible is done. So there's a few things we can still do in this bill. We have also heard Aadu Black's observations here that there are a number of clerical errors in the draft, which should be corrected. I think we could slow down right now. On behalf of the Centre Party Group, I propose that the second reading of this draft be interrupted, so that we have time to review it once more and to make a quite proper draft that will protect the prisoners and clarify the situation of the prison guards. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U1247	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, The draft we are discussing today is part of this package of laws, which is subject to a high level of public attention. This is precisely because the problems of imprisonment involve a number of questions about people's sense of justice. Taxpayers are often annoyed by the fact that prisoners are supported by taxpayers' money and not by the work of prisoners themselves. On the other hand, we try to be democrats, we try to show that we are a family of developed countries. Just as we are between the hammer and the anvils, trying to find a delicate compromise where, on the one hand, the purpose of imprisonment is met, but on the other, we cannot ignore that social justice is an important category. Very exciting discussions have taken place in Scandinavian countries in this area. I looked at the materials that they say about putting prisoners into work. It turns out that not all experiences have turned out to be gold either. For example, in Denmark, it was written that all prisoners should be employed. The prison office became famous for the fact that the prisoners were even involved in the work of the country's most famous furniture company. 'How is that possible? " The Finns distressed it. It took them at least two years to train a skilled furniture worker, but Danish prisons, where prisoners sat for half or three quarters of a year, boasted that their prisoners were already making furniture. When the journalists were sent in, it became clear that the prisoners mostly only went over the finished furniture with a clean cloth, because there was no further release. However, it must be said that the Finns have set a good example for us in this matter and have done a very big job. In Finland, the prison industry has been applied to the restoration of cultural monuments, especially building monuments. These jobs are precisely the work of prison labour-based companies. At first, there were serious disputes about this in Finland. Officials were charged that they were instigating the market, private operators were unable to compete with such price competition, such dumping. However, at the end of the day, there was a social agreement that this area would be out of competition on the free market and that it would work for the benefit of national cultural heritage, financed by public money. A commendable compromise. We could move towards something similar, despite the policies of our government, which promotes the ultimate liberality and the market economy. Employment in prisons could be greater not only because prisoners earn money, but also because of the regime, order and security. The old truth about prison officers tells me that if you don't give the prisoner enough time, you're gonna have to deal with him very, very, very, very painfully later. We may have the most implemented prisoners in the informant's office, for which these prisoners also receive certain benefits. It's a good thing they're doing this, some kind of action. That last sentence was a humor. When we look into the lists of involvement of prisoners in various activities, we meet an interesting pattern. The number of activities in prisons is quite high and the number of people involved in each activity is low. Let me give you an example. All the names in this example are made up, and their interaction with real people is random. Murulauk, Petersell and Onion participate in the prison basketball ring. Petersell, Onion, Chives and Strawberry participate in the volleyball ring. The evening school is attended by Murulauk, Strawberry, Petersell and Onions. More time, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-30_U1248	Inmate Vanker, Tõld, Troska, Saan and Sledk do not go to basketball ring, volleyball ring, evening school, vocational training or anywhere. This is not a random example, however, but an example that describes Estonia's circumstances quite precisely. They are dealt with in prisons and are cultivated by those who are "soft" and "soft." Less soft people deal with their own business, such as managing their legal or, in some cases, illegal businesses directly between prison walls. The latter can be done, for example, by letter, but why not by telephone. Contacts with the neighborhood are an integral part of prison life, and maybe it's not bad. Here, the presentation of our esteemed Prime Minister in Question Time has already been recalled. You remember him saying from here: 'Building a prison in Ida-Virumaa is a regional project. ' But he also continued: 'It may be ironized, but it is also important for prisoners to maintain a bond with their loved ones. If the prisoner stays in touch with the essential people at large, it is less likely to return to criminal life. This is confirmed by statistics from all over the world. " The fact that the statistics of the whole world confirm this is, of course, a belletistic exaggeration characteristic of Ansip, but he has understood the connection of prisoners to the surrounding area correctly, it is quite there. The bill in question contains a lot of talk about prisoners' affairs, their right to possess them, or their disqualification, which is also a painful subject for prisons. All prison officials know that the fewer prisoners have things, the better the prison order. There are examples of how living in prisons has been quite like a storage room. There's been a hint that a soldier is a completely free man, he can't have a lot of things, but he can have a prisoner. This is again a sensitive issue. Certainly, moving various workshops into the prison chamber should be a thing that should not be favorable. Why can't people who want to draw sit in a special room and draw in it? If drawing is allowed in a cell, you will soon see that playing cards are constantly being drawn, sold and released for gambling. With this bill, we regulate some of the details related to imprisonment, make lessons in detention. Solving prison and prison-related problems is likely to remain in the future. I'm sure we'll be on it soon. Thank you.
